R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{d_{1}+(3n+k)(d_{3}+1)}y_{0}^{d_{2}+(m-k+1)(d_{3}+1)-3d_{5}}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}+a_{k,j}x_{j})^{d_{5}}.

Define
s:=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
as before. Set the
d_{i}
’s as specified in Section
3.5.4
to satisfy the conditions in (
5
). Define
f
as:

f({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{d_{1}}y_{0}^{d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}).

(11)

Observation
3.4
holds with little change. Observation
3.7
analyses
{\mathcal{S}}(f)
and
\textnormal{Supp}(f)
.

Observation 3.7
.

{\mathcal{S}}(f)\leq 1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{3}
and
5\leq\textnormal{Supp}(f)\leq 8
.

Remarks.

Like the non-homogeneous case, over characteristic
2
fields, the sparsity of the homogeneous polynomial output by the reduction depends on the number of variables which are complemented within a clause. Hence, for the same number of variables
n
and same number of clauses
m
, the output polynomial corresponding to two different
\psi
’s may have different sparsity. Hence, the reduction is not natural over characteristic
2
fields.

The forward direction.

Let
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be such that
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
and
f
, as described in (
11
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
. Proposition
3.7
shows how
{\mathbf{u}}
can be used to construct a sparsifying transform. The proof of Proposition
3.7
is similar to that of Proposition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.7
.

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s
where
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
is as follows:

A:x_{0}\mapsto x_{0},~y_{0}\mapsto y_{0},~x_{i}\mapsto x_{i},~y_{i}\mapsto y_{i}+(1-u_{i})x_{i}\ \ \forall i\in[n].

(12)

The reverse direction.

Let
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
be such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s
. Lemmas
3.4
and
3.5
hold with little change in the arguments presented in the finite characteristic case. Thus,
A(x_{0})=x_{0}
and
A(y_{0})=y_{0}
without loss of generality. Lemma
3.9
analyses
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}(A{\mathbf{z}}))
,
i\in[n]
, and its proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.6
.

Lemma 3.9
.

For any invertible
A
and
i\in[n]
:

{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}(A{\mathbf{z}}))={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}(A{\mathbf{z}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}(A{\mathbf{z}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,3}(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{4}+3,

where
Q_{i}
,
Q_{i,1}
,
Q_{i,2}
and
Q_{i,3}
are as defined in this subsection. Equality holds if and only if under
A

x_{i}\mapsto X_{i}\text{ and }y_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}+(1-u_{i})X_{i}

for some scaled
X_{i},Y_{i}\in{\mathbf{z}}
and
u_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. Further, if
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}(A{\mathbf{z}}))\neq d_{4}+3
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq 2d_{4}+3
.

Lemma
3.7
also holds with the same proof as before. Lemmas
3.4
,
3.9
and
3.7
together show that
A
is a permuted scaled version of the transform described in (
12
). Proposition
3.8
then holds and can be proven similarly to Proposition
3.4
.

Proposition 3.8
.

With
A
as described in (
12
),
{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a satisfying assignment for
\psi
.

3.5.4
Setting of parameters in the homogeneous case

Let the characteristic be
p>0
. If
p>d_{1}+d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1)
, where
d_{1},d_{2}
and
d_{3}
are as chosen in (
6
) for the characteristic
0
case, then the same setting of
d_{i}
’s holds. Otherwise,
p=O((n+m)m^{3})
. Similar to the non-homogeneous case, we choose
d_{3}
,
d_{4}
and
d_{5}
so that
d_{3}>d_{4}>d_{5}
and they are of form
p^{k}-1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We have the following bounds:

\begin{split}d_{5}&\leq pm\implies d_{5}=O(pm)=O((n+m)m^{4}),\\
d_{4}&\leq pm(d_{5}+1)^{2}+p\implies d_{4}=O(p^{3}m^{3})=O((n+m)^{3}m^{12}),\\
\therefore s&=O(p^{3}nm^{3})=O((n+m)^{3}nm^{12}),\\
d_{3}&=pd_{4}+p-1\implies d_{3}=O(p^{4}m^{3})=O((n+m)^{4}m^{15}).\end{split}

(13)

This choice of
d_{3}
,
d_{4}
and
d_{5}
ensures they are
(mn)^{O(1)}
, satisfy (
5
) and that Observation
2.5
can be used for characteristic
p
fields with
p=O((n+m)m^{3})
. Now, let
k_{1}:=\lfloor\log_{p}(s)\rfloor+1,k_{2}:=\lfloor\log_{p}((3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1))\rfloor+1
, then set

d_{2}=\sum_{i=k_{2}}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-1}(p-1)p^{i}=p^{k_{1}+k_{2}}-p^{k_{2}}.

(14)

For this choice,
d_{2}>s
and
d_{2}>(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1)
. Lastly, let
k_{3}:=\lfloor\log_{p}(d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1))\rfloor+1
, then set

d_{1}=\sum_{i=k_{3}}^{k_{3}+k_{1}-1}(p-1)p^{i}=p^{k_{1}+k_{3}}-p^{k_{3}}>d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1).

(15)

For this choice of
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
it holds that,

d_{2}=O(p^{2}s(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1))=O(p^{9}nm^{6}(n+m))=O((n+m)^{10}nm^{33})

d_{1}=O(p^{2}s(d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1)))=O(p^{14}n^{2}m^{9}(n+m))=O((n+m)^{15}n^{2}m^{51}).

4
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}

In this section, we prove Theorem
2
. We first prove parts
1
and
2
of Theorem
2
over characteristic
0
fields without considering translations in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, respectively. Section
4.3
extends both parts to finite characteristic fields. Appendix
D
contains the proofs of the lemmas in this section. Appendix
A.2
proves part
1
of Theorem
2
while considering translations.
19
19

19

For part
2
of Theorem
2
, translations are not considered; see footnote
17
for an explanation.

Proof sketch.

For a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
, we carefully analyze the sparsity of the corresponding polynomial
f
as defined in Section
3
, for the non-homogeneous and the homogeneous case. For unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, we do a slightly deeper analysis on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
, for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
, to show a lower bound. For satisfiable
\psi
’s,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
has already been upper bounded for an appropriate
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. The degree parameters are also chosen differently so that the gap between the lower bound and the upper bound is significant. Comparing the sparsities for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s proves Theorem
2
. Note that throughout this section, we assume
\epsilon\in(0,1/3)
to be an arbitrary constant.

4.1
Analyzing the gap: the non-homogeneous case

For a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
, consider the polynomial
f
as defined in (
1
). Choose the
d_{i}
’s to satisfy the following while also being
(mn)^{O(1)}
:

d_{4}\geq\max(4mn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{3}=m(d_{4}+1)^{2}+1,\ d_{2}=d_{3}^{2}+1,\ d_{1}=d_{2}+1.

(16)

Note under these constraints, the conditions in (
2
) are also satisfied. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Then,
s=1+n(2d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{3}
by Observation
3.3
. For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.1
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. In the lemma, Item
1
is essentially Lemma
3.1
, Items
2
and
3
are a slightly deeper analysis of that in Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
, and Item
4
is the analysis in Proposition
3.2
. Thus, Lemma
4.1
encapsulates the analysis of Section
3.3
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.1
, there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.1
shows
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by comparing the sparsity for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, and uses Lemma
4.1
and the conditions in (
16
).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
be as defined in (
1
) corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{1}+1
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables for some
j\in[n]
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{2}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in items
1
and
2
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j}-x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\frac{d_{3}^{2}+3d_{3}+2}{2}
.

4.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous three items, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{4}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
4
). If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, then it follows from Lemma
4.1
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min\bigg(d_{1}+1,d_{2}+1,\frac{d_{3}^{2}+3d_{3}+2}{2},(d_{4}+1)^{3}\bigg).

The constraints imposed in (
16
) ensure that
(d_{4}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{3}=m(d_{4}+1)^{2}+1
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{3}=3mn(d_{4}+1)^{2}+3n\leq 4mn(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and the NO instances is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{4mn(d_{4}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{4}+1}{4mn}.

Also, as
d_{4}\geq 4mn
,
{{\mathcal{S}}(f)}=s\leq 2m(d_{4}+1)^{3}\implies d_{4}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{4}+1}{4mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{4}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

4.2
Analyzing the gap: the homogeneous case

Consider the polynomial
f
as defined in (
7
) for
\psi
. Choose the
d_{i}
’s to satisfy the following constraints while also being
(mn)^{O(1)}
.

\begin{split}d_{5}&\geq\max((4mn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{4}=m(d_{5}+1)^{2}+1,\ d_{3}=d_{4}^{2}+1,\\
d_{2}&=\max((3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1),s)+1,\\
d_{1}&=d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1)+1.\end{split}

(17)

Under these constraints, the conditions in (
5
) are also satisfied. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Then,
s=1+n(2d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{3}
by Observation
3.5
. For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.2
, proved using Claim
2.1
, shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
, for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. In the lemma, Items
1
and
2
are essentially Lemma
3.4
, Items
3
and
4
are a deeper analysis of that in Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
, and Item
5
is the analysis in Proposition
3.4
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.3
, there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.2
proves
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard using Lemma
4.2
and the conditions in (
17
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f({\mathbf{z}})
be the polynomial as defined in (
7
) corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{1}+1
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
A(y_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{2}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in items
1
and
2
, and
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables for some
j\in[n]
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{3}+1
.

4.

If
A
is not as in items
1
,
2
and
3
, and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j}-x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\frac{d_{4}^{2}+3d_{4}+2}{2}
.

5.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous four items, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{5}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse homogeneous polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
8
). If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, then it follows from Lemma
4.2
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min\bigg(d_{1}+1,d_{2}+1,d_{3}+1,\frac{d_{4}^{2}+3d_{4}+2}{2},(d_{5}+1)^{3}\bigg).

The constraints imposed in (
17
) ensure that
(d_{5}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{4}=m(d_{5}+1)^{2}+1
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{5}=3mn(d_{5}+1)^{2}+3n\leq 4mn(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and the NO instances is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{4mn(d_{5}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{5}+1}{4mn}.

Also, as
d_{5}\geq 4mn
,
{{\mathcal{S}}(f)}=s\leq 2m(d_{5}+1)^{3}\implies d_{5}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{5}+1}{4mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{5}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

Remarks.

As mentioned in the second remark near the end of Section
3.4
, a simpler construction of a homogeneous
f
with four degree parameters is possible. This simpler construction can be used to show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
. The reason we use the construction currently defined in Section
3.4
is that it allows us to prove part
2
of Theorem
2
for homogeneous polynomials over finite characteristic fields and characteristic
0
fields without using separate constructions.

4.3
Extension to finite characteristic fields

In this section, we will show how the construction of Sections
3.1
and
3.4
, with some changes for appropriate cases, can be used to show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
over finite characteristic fields for the non-homogeneous case (in the following section) and homogeneous case (in Section
4.3.2
), respectively.

4.3.1
The non-homogeneous case

Let the characteristic be
p
, where
p>2
. If
p>d_{1}
, where
d_{1}
is as chosen in Section
4.1
, then the argument of that section holds. Hence, it is assumed that
p\leq d_{1}=(mn)^{O(1)}
. Consider the polynomial
f
defined in (
1
). Let
\epsilon\in(0,1/3)
be an arbitrary constant. Choose the
d_{i}
’s to be of form
p^{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
to satisfy the following inequalities, along with those of (
2
):

d_{4}\geq\max(3pmn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{3}>m(d_{4}+1)^{2},\ d_{2}>(d_{3}+1)^{2},\ d_{1}>d_{2}.

(18)

Note that we can get
d_{3}=O(pm(d_{4}+1)^{2})
,
d_{2}=O(p(d_{3}+1)^{2})
and
d_{1}=O(pd_{2})
. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Then,
s=1+n(2d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{3}
by Observation
3.3
. For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.3
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. Like Lemma
4.1
, Lemma
4.3
is a slightly deeper analysis of that in the reverse direction of Section
3.5.1
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.1
there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.3
shows the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
using Lemma
4.3
and the inequalities in (
18
).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
, as defined in (
1
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{1}+1
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{2}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
2
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j}-x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{3}+1)^{1.63}
.

4.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous three cases, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{4}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=p>2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.1
. If
\psi
is satisfiable, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
4
) and
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. For unsatisfiable
\psi
, it follows from Lemma
4.3
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min(d_{1}+1,d_{2}+1,(d_{3}+1)^{1.63},(d_{4}+1)^{3}).

As
d_{3}>m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{3}\leq 3pmn(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. The conditions imposed in (
18
) ensure that
d_{1}+1>d_{2}+1>(d_{3}+1)^{1+\log_{p}((p+1)/2)}>(d_{4}+1)^{3}>s_{0}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and NO instances is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{3pmn(d_{4}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{4}+1}{3pmn}.

Also, note as
d_{4}\geq 3pmn
, therefore
s\leq 2m(d_{4}+1)^{3}\implies d_{4}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{4}+1}{3pmn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{4}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

Analysis over characteristic
2
fields

In this case, we consider the construction of Section
3.5.1
. For a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
, let
f
be the corresponding polynomial as defined in (
9
). Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Over characteristic
2
fields, the value of
s
depends on the number of variables complemented in a clause. To show the hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
, we require
s\geq d_{4}^{3}
(see the proof of Proposition
4.4
). This can be achieved if there exists a clause with all variables complemented. Hence, we assume, without loss of generality, that there is such a clause in
\psi
.
20
20

20

To have some clause, say the first one, contain only complemented variables, every uncomplemented variable
x
in the clause can be replaced by
\neg x
followed by complementing each occurrence of
x
in the remaining clauses.
The
d_{i}
’s are chosen in the same way as in Section
4.3.1
to satisfy (
18
) with
p
set to
2
. In particular, they satisfy the following inequalities.

d_{4}\geq\max(6mn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{3}>m(d_{4}+1)^{2},\ d_{2}>(d_{3}+1)^{2},\ d_{1}>d_{2}.

(19)

By Observation
3.6
and the assumption on
\psi
, it holds that

1+n(d_{3}+3)+(d_{4}+1)^{3}\leq s\leq 1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{3}.

For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.4
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
where
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.5
there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.4
shows the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
using Lemma
4.4
and the inequalities in (
19
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
, as defined in (
9
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{1}+1
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{2}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
2
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{3}+1)^{1.58}
.

4.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous three cases, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{4}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.3
. If
\psi
is satisfiable, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
4
) and
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. For unsatisfiable
\psi
, it follows from Lemma
4.3
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min(d_{1}+1,d_{2}+1,(d_{3}+1)^{1.58},(d_{4}+1)^{3}).

As
d_{3}>m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{3}\leq 6mn(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. The conditions imposed in (
19
) ensure that
d_{1}+1>d_{2}+1>(d_{3}+1)^{1.58}>(d_{4}+1)^{3}>s_{0}
. Consequently, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and NO instances is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{6mn(d_{4}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{4}+1}{6mn}.

Also, note as
d_{4}\geq 6mn
, therefore
s\leq 2m(d_{4}+1)^{3}\implies d_{4}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{4}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{4}+1}{6mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{4}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{4}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

4.3.2
The homogeneous case

Let the characteristic be
p
, where
p>2
. If
p>d_{1}
, where
d_{1}
is as chosen in Section
4.2
, then the argument of that section holds. Hence, assume
p\leq d_{1}=O((mn)^{O(1)})
. Consider the polynomial
f
defined in (
7
). Choose
d_{3}
,
d_{4}
and
d_{5}
to be of form
p^{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
while also satisfying:

\begin{split}d_{5}&\geq\max(3pmn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{4}>m(d_{5}+1)^{2},\ d_{3}>(d_{4}+1)^{2}.\end{split}

(20)

Thus,
d_{4}=O(pm(d_{5}+1)^{2})
and
d_{3}=O(p(d_{4}+1)^{2})
. Now, let
k_{1}:=\lfloor\log_{p}(d_{3}+2)\rfloor+1,k_{2}:=\lfloor\log_{p}((3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1))\rfloor+1
, then set

d_{2}:=\sum_{i=k_{2}}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-1}(p-1)p^{i}=p^{k_{1}+k_{2}}-p^{k_{2}}.

(21)

Lastly, let
k_{3}:=\lfloor\log_{p}(d_{2}+(3n+m+1)(d_{3}+1))\rfloor+1
, then set

d_{1}:=\sum_{i=k_{3}}^{k_{3}+k_{1}-1}(p-1)p^{i}=p^{k_{1}+k_{3}}-p^{k_{3}}.

(22)

For this choice of the
d_{i}
’s, the conditions in (
5
) are satisfied. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. By Observation
3.5
,
s=1+n(2d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{3}
. For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.5
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. Like Lemma
4.2
, Lemma
4.5
is a slightly deeper analysis of that in the reverse direction of Section
3.5.3
For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.3
there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.5
shows the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
using Lemma
4.5
and the setting of the
d_{i}
’s in this section.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
, as defined in (
7
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
or
A(y_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{3}+2
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables for some
j\in[n]
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{3}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in items
1
and
2
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j}-x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{4}+1)^{1.63}
.

4.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous three cases, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{5}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=p>2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse homogeneous polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.2
. If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
8
). If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, then it follows from Lemma
4.3
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min\bigg(d_{3}+2,d_{3}+1,(d_{4}+1)^{1.63},(d_{5}+1)^{3}\bigg).

The constraints imposed in (
16
) ensure that
(d_{5}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{4}>m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}<3nd_{4}\leq 3pmn(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and the NO instances is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{3pmn(d_{5}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{5}+1}{3pmn}.

Also, as
d_{5}\geq 3pmn
,
{{\mathcal{S}}(f)}=s\leq 2m(d_{5}+1)^{3}\implies d_{5}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{5}+1}{3pmn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{5}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

Analysis over characteristic
2
fields

For characteristic
2
fields, consider the polynomial
f
as defined in (
11
). Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Like in the characteristic
2
construction for the non-homogeneous case, the value of
s
depends on the number of variables complemented within a clause. To show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
,
s\geq d_{5}^{3}
is required (see the proof of Proposition
4.6
), which can be achieved if there is at least one clause where all variables are complemented. Therefore, we assume that such a clause exists (see footnote
20
). Set the
d_{i}
’s as specified in the beginning of Section
4.3.2
with
p=2
. For this setting of the
d_{i}
’s, the constraints in (
5
) are satisfied. By Observation
3.7
and the assumption on
\psi
, it holds that

1+n(d_{4}+3)+(d_{5}+1)^{3}\leq s\leq 1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{3}.

For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
4.6
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))
for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
3.7
there exists
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
4.6
shows the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
using Lemma
4.6
and the setting of the
d_{i}
’s in this section.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
, as defined in (
11
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
and
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
.

1.

If
A(x_{0})
or
A(y_{0})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{3}+2
.

2.

If
A
is not as in item
1
and
A(x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
2
variables for some
j\in[n]
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq d_{3}+1
.

3.

If
A
is not as in items
1
and
2
and for some
j\in[n]
,
A(y_{j}+x_{j})
or
A(y_{j}-x_{j})
is a linear form in at least
3
variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{4}+1)^{1.58}
.

4.

If
A
is not of the form described in the previous three cases, then

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq(d_{5}+1)^{3}.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse homogeneous polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
4.5
. If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\leq s_{0}
where
A
is as described in (
12
). If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, then it follows from Lemma
4.3
that for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f(A{\mathbf{z}}))\geq\min\bigg(d_{3}+2,d_{3}+1,(d_{4}+1)^{1.58},(d_{5}+1)^{3}\bigg).

The constraints imposed in (
16
) ensure that
(d_{5}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{4}>m(d_{5}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{4}+3)+m(d_{5}+1)^{2}<3nd_{4}\leq 6mn(d_{5}+1)^{2}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and the NO instances is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{6mn(d_{5}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{5}+1}{6mn}.

Also, as
d_{5}\geq 6mn
,
s\leq 2m(d_{5}+1)^{3}\implies d_{5}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{5}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{5}+1}{6mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{5}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{5}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

5
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\mathrm{ETsupport}

In this section, we prove Theorem
3
. All lemmas and observations are proved in Appendix
E
. As stated at the end of Section
1.3
, we strengthen the construction in
[
11
]
to prove Theorem
3
for
\sigma\geq 5
. At the end of Section
5.1
, we describe the differences between the current construction and that in
[
11
]
.

Proof sketch.

We map
\psi
, a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}
, to a polynomial
f
, which is the sum of polynomials which are degree separated or degree separated with respect to some variable. At least one polynomial in
f
is of support
\sigma+1
and the rest of support
\sigma
(where
\sigma
is a constant). As the summands are degree separated or degree separated with respect to some variable,
\textnormal{Supp}(f)=\sigma+1
and for any invertible linear transform
A
,
\textnormal{Supp}(A(f))
is equal to the maximum support size among the transformed summands. Claim
2.2
is used to show that
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
iff there exists an invertible linear transform
A
, such that
\textnormal{Supp}(A(f))\leq\sigma
. Thus, the reduction also holds for
(\sigma+1)\mathrm{\text{-}to\text{-}}\sigma\mathrm{\ ETsupport}
. For characteristic
p
fields, we assume
p>\sigma+1
so that Claim
2.2
holds.

5.1
Construction of
f

Let
\sigma\geq 5
be an even integer constant and
\psi
be as denoted in Section
3.1
. For odd
\sigma
, we describe the changes in the construction/argument at the appropriate points. As
\sigma
is a constant, let
n\geq\sigma+4
. The proofs of Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
use
n\geq\sigma+4
. To ensure
\textnormal{Supp}(f)=\sigma+1
, we assume that all the variables in the first clause are complemented (see footnote
20
) and that no clause is repeated. Let
{\mathbf{x}}:=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
,
{\mathbf{y}}:=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
and
{\mathbf{z}}:=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{\sigma-5}\}
and
{\mathbf{w}}:={\mathbf{x}}\sqcup{\mathbf{y}}\sqcup{\mathbf{z}}
. Note that when
\sigma=5
,
{\mathbf{z}}
is an empty set and hence we do not consider the
{\mathbf{z}}
variables in such a case. By
(w_{1}\cdots w_{l})^{\star}
, where
w_{i}\in{\mathbf{w}}
and
l,\star\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote a power of
w_{1}\cdots w_{l}
. Consider the polynomials:

•

First, introduce
\binom{n+\sigma-5}{\sigma}
many monomials defined by the set

P:=\{(w_{1}\cdots w_{{\sigma}})^{\star}\ |\ w_{1},\dots,w_{{\sigma}}\in{\mathbf{z}}\sqcup{\mathbf{x}}\ \text{ and are pairwise distinct}\}.

•

Then, introduce
\binom{n}{\frac{\sigma}{2}}
many monomials defined by the set

Q:=\{((x_{i_{1}}y_{i_{1}})\cdots(x_{i_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}y_{i_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}))^{\star}\ |\ i_{1},\dots,i_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\in[n]\ \text{ and are pairwise distinct}\}.

Note:
For odd
\sigma
, the monomials are of form
((x_{i_{1}}y_{i_{1}})\cdots(x_{i_{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}}y_{i_{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}})x_{i_{\frac{\sigma+1}{2}}})^{\star}
. Thus
|Q|=\binom{n}{\frac{\sigma+1}{2}}\frac{\sigma+1}{2}
.

•

Let
R:=\{R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})\ |\ k\in[m]\}
, where
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
is defined corresponding to the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause as:

R_{k}({\mathbf{w}}):=(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}-a_{k,j}x_{j})^{2+a_{k,j}})(z_{1}\cdots z_{\sigma-5}).

Define
f({\mathbf{w}}):=\sum_{g\in P}g({\mathbf{w}})+\sum_{h\in Q}h({\mathbf{w}})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
. The powers, denoted by
\star
, must be such that the following hold:

1.

All the polynomials in
P\sqcup Q
are degree separated from one another and from the
R_{k}
’s, with the powers being at least
\sigma+1
.

2.

Over characteristic
p
fields, where
p>0
, all the powers are less than
p
or are of the form
p^{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

In Section
E.6
, we choose the powers to satisfy the above conditions over any field. The following observations hold by the definition of the polynomials and the above conditions.

Observation 5.1
.

For
k\in[m]
, the degree of
R_{k}
is at most
\sigma+4
and
\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k}({\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma+1
with equality in both cases when
a_{k,j}=1
for all
j\in C_{k}
. For
k,l\in[m]
with
l\neq k
, any monomial of
R_{k}
is degree separated from every monomial of
R_{l}
with respect to some variable in
{\mathbf{x}}\sqcup{\mathbf{y}}
.

Observation 5.2
.

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{w}}))=O(n^{\sigma}+m)
and
\textnormal{Supp}(f({\mathbf{w}}))=\sigma+1
.

Observation 5.3
.

For any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{w}}|,\mathbb{F})
, it holds that

\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))=\max\left(\max_{g\in P,h\in Q}\big(\textnormal{Supp}(g(A{\mathbf{w}})),\textnormal{Supp}(h(A{\mathbf{w}}))\big),\textnormal{Supp}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}})\right)\right).

Note:
There are three differences between the current construction and that in
[
11
]
:

1.

In
[
11
]
, the clause polynomial
R_{k}
was defined as:

R_{k}({\mathbf{w}}):=(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}-a_{k,j}x_{j})^{2})(z_{1}z_{2}\dots z_{\sigma-5})^{\star}.

The monomial
(z_{1}z_{2}\dots z_{\sigma-5})^{\star}
was used to make the
R_{k}
’s degree separated from one another and from the polynomials in
P\sqcup Q
(by assigning appropriate values to the powers
\star
), and to ensure that some
R_{k}
has support
\sigma+1
. The former condition necessitated imposing
\sigma\geq 6
. In the current construction, we leverage the fact that a variable can be complemented or uncomplemented in a clause to make the
R_{k}
’s degree separated from one another with respect to some variable in
{\mathbf{x}}\sqcup{\mathbf{y}}
(see Observation
5.1
) regardless of the presence or absence of the monomial
z_{1}z_{2}\dots z_{\sigma-5}
, which improves
\sigma\geq 6
to
\sigma\geq 5
. We still need
z_{1}z_{2}\dots z_{\sigma-5}
in the
R_{k}
’s to ensure some
R_{k}
has support
\sigma+1
when
\sigma\geq 6
.

2.

In
[
11
]
, Condition
1
stated that all polynomials in
P\sqcup Q\sqcup R
are degree separated with the powers being at least
\sigma+1
, which implied that
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))
, for any
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{w}}|,\mathbb{F})
can be analyzed by analyzing the support of individual polynomials. In contrast, the condition is now less restrictive as the nature of the polynomials in
P\sqcup Q
is such that if these polynomials are degree separated amongst themselves and from the
R_{k}^{\prime}s
, and if
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{w}}|,\mathbb{F})
is such that
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
, then
A
has a certain structure as implied by Observation
5.3
and Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
. The structured nature of
A
implies that
R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}})
looks similar to
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
and then an argument similar to the proof of Observation
5.1
shows that it suffices to analyze the support of individual
R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}})
’s which leads to the recovery of a satisfying assignment (see the proof of Proposition
5.2
).

3.

In
[
11
]
, the values assigned to the powers,
\star
, were dependent on
m
(the number of clauses), while in this version the powers are independent of
m
.

5.2
The forward direction

Proposition
5.1
shows how a satisfying assignment for
\psi
implies the existence of an invertible
A
, such that
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))=\sigma
, by constructing
A
from the satisfying assignment.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
with
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
a satisfying assignment. Then,
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))=\sigma
, where the transform
A
is defined as

\begin{split}A:z_{j}\mapsto z_{j},\ x_{i}\mapsto x_{i},\ y_{i}\mapsto y_{i}+(1-u_{i})x_{i}\ \ i\in[n],j\in[\sigma-5].\end{split}

(23)

Proof.

As Condition
1
and Observation
5.3
are satisfied, it suffices to analyse the action of
A
on individual polynomials of
P\sqcup Q
and then analyse
\textnormal{Supp}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))
. Clearly, for
g({\mathbf{w}})\in P
,
g(A{\mathbf{w}})=g({\mathbf{w}})
. Let
h({\mathbf{w}})\in Q
. Then
h({\mathbf{w}})
is of form
((x_{t_{1}}y_{t_{1}})\cdots(x_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}y_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}))^{\star}
,
t_{j}\in[n]
, and
A
acts on
h
as:

A:((x_{t_{1}}y_{t_{1}})\cdots(x_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}y_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}))^{\star}\mapsto((x_{t_{1}})(y_{t_{1}}+(1-u_{t_{1}})x_{t_{1}})\cdots(x_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}})(y_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}+(1-u_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}})x_{t_{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}))^{\star}.

Note
|\cup_{t=1}^{\sigma}\mathrm{var}(\ell_{t})|=\sigma
, where
\ell_{t}=A(w)
and
w\in\mathrm{var}(h({\mathbf{w}}))
. Thus,
\textnormal{Supp}(h(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
. When
\sigma
is odd, a similar argument holds for the modified construction of
Q
. For
k\in[m]
,
A
acts on
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
as:

A:(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}-a_{k,j}x_{j})^{2+a_{k,j}})\cdot(z_{1}\cdots z_{\sigma-5})\mapsto(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}+(1-a_{k,j}-u_{j})x_{j})^{2+a_{k,j}}\cdot(z_{1}\cdots z_{\sigma-5}).

If
a_{k,j}\neq u_{j}
, then
a_{k,j}=1-u_{j}
. Since
\psi
is satisfiable, therefore for all
k\in[m]
,
a_{k,j}\neq u_{j}
for some
j\in C_{k}
. Hence,
\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq(\sigma-5)+5=\sigma
for all
k\in[m]
. This implies
\textnormal{Supp}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
. Thus,
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))=\sigma
.
∎

5.3
The reverse direction

Now, we show that if
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
for
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{w}}|,\mathbb{F})
, then a satisfying assignment can be recovered for
\psi
. Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
, proved using Claim
2.2
, together show that
A
is as:

A:z_{j}\mapsto z_{j},\ x_{i}\mapsto x_{i},\ y_{i}\mapsto y_{i}+c_{i}x_{i}\ \ \ c_{i}\in\mathbb{F},\ j\in[\sigma-5],i\in[n]

without loss of generality.
21
21

21

as permutation and non-zero scaling of variables do not affect the support.
Proposition
5.2
derives a satisfying assignment for
\psi
from
A
.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
, then
\forall w\in{\mathbf{z}}\sqcup{\mathbf{x}}
,
A(w)=W
, for scaled variable
W\in{\mathbf{w}}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
, then
A(x_{i})=X_{i}
and
A(y_{i})=Y_{i}+c_{i}X_{i}
, for scaled variables
Y_{i},X_{i}\in{\mathbf{w}}
and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{F}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

A satisfying assignment
{\mathbf{u}}
for
\psi
can be extracted from
A
.

Proof.

The action of
A
on
R_{k}
, where
k\in[m]
, is:

(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}-a_{k,j}x_{j})^{2+a_{k,j}})\cdot(z_{1}\cdots z_{\sigma-5})\mapsto(\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}+(c_{j}-a_{k,j})x_{j})^{2+a_{k,j}})\cdot(z_{1}\cdots z_{\sigma-5}).

An argument similar to the proof of Observation
5.1
shows that for any
k,l\in[m]
with
k\neq l
, any monomial of
R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}})
is degree separated from every monomial of
R_{l}(A{\mathbf{w}})
with respect to some variable in
{\mathbf{x}}\sqcup{\mathbf{y}}
. This implies
\textnormal{Supp}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))=\max_{k\in[m]}(\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))
. Since
\textnormal{Supp}(f(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
, therefore
\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
for all
k\in[m]
. Using the fact that the characteristic of the field is not equal to
2
or
3
and the action of
A
of
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
, it can be seen that
\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k}(A{\mathbf{w}}))\leq\sigma
iff for some
j\in C_{k}
,
c_{j}=a_{k,j}
. Hence, for each
R_{k}({\mathbf{w}})
, there exists
j\in C_{k}
such that
c_{j}\in\{0,1\}
. Construct
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
by setting
u_{j}:=1-c_{j}
, for appropriate
j\in C_{k}
and the remaining
u_{i}
’s to arbitrary values in
\{0,1\}
. From the definition of
{\mathbf{u}}
, it follows that for the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause, there exists
j\in C_{k}
such that
u_{j}\neq a_{k,j}
. As
k
is arbitrary, all clauses are satisfied.
∎

6
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\mathrm{SETsparse}
and
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}

In Section
6.1
, we adapt the reduction and analysis of Section
3
to prove part one of Theorem
4
. In Section
6.2
, we prove part two of Theorem
4
by an analysis similar to that in Section
4
. Note that for uniformity of presentation, we show the reduction and analysis over fields; the same also holds over any integral domain. In Section
6.3
, we briefly describe the reduction in
[
18
]
and then compare our results with theirs. The proofs of the observations and lemmas in this section can be found in Appendix
F
.

6.1
Hardness of
\mathrm{SETsparse}

Proof sketch.

The reduction is similar to that in Section
3
. Each variable and clause of a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
is mapped to a distinct polynomial and these polynomials are degree separated from one another with respect to a fixed variable. Summing these polynomials gives the polynomial
f
. The degrees are chosen such that
f
is shift-equivalent to an
s
-sparse polynomial (for a suitable sparsity parameter
s
) if and only if
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
. We first describe the reduction for fields
\mathbb{F}
of characteristic not equal to
2
and then do so for characteristic
2
fields.

Formally, let
\psi
,
{\mathbf{x}}
and
x_{0}
be as denoted in Section
3
. Let
{\mathbf{z}}:={\mathbf{x}}\sqcup\{x_{0}\}
. For
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
,
{\mathbf{b}}_{|w}
denotes the component of
{\mathbf{b}}
corresponding to the variable
w\in{\mathbf{z}}
. Let
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
,
d_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the following polynomials:

•

Corresponding to
x_{i}
, where
i\in[n]
, define
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
as:

\begin{split}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})&:=Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})+Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}),\ Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)}(x_{i}+1)^{d_{2}}\text{ and }\\
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})&:=x_{0}^{2i(d_{2}+1)}(x_{i}-1)^{d_{2}}.\end{split}

•

For the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause,
k\in[m]
, define
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{k}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}})^{d_{3}}.

Define
s:=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Impose the following conditions on the
d_{i}
’s:

d_{1}\geq 4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2,\ d_{2}\geq m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+1,\ d_{3}\geq m.

(24)

Finally, define
f({\mathbf{z}})
as:

f({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{d_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}).

(25)

The
d_{i}
’s are chosen in Section
6.1.1
such that they are
(mn)^{O(1)}
while also satisfying the inequalities of (
24
). Observations
6.1
,
6.2
and
6.3
hold under the conditions of (
24
).

Observation 6.1
.

For all
i\in[n]
,
k\in[m]
, the polynomials
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
,
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})
,
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})
and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
are degree separated from one another with respect to
x_{0}
. Also,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from other
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}})
’s with respect to
x_{0}
, for
i,j\in[n]
and
i\neq j
. Similarly,
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}})
with respect to
x_{0}
for
k,l\in[m]
and
k\neq l
.

Observation 6.2
.

The degree of
f
is
d_{1}=(mn)^{O(1)}
. Also,
\frac{d_{1}}{2}>s
.

Observation 6.3
.

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}))=1+n(2d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}
and
\textnormal{Supp}(f)=4
.

The forward direction.

If
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is such that
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
and
f
, as described in (
25
), is the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
, then Proposition
6.1
shows that there exists a
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
, defined using
{\mathbf{u}}
, such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
holds.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, such that
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
. Then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
, where
{\mathbf{b}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
is defined as follows:

{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}:=0,{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}:=(-1)^{u_{i}},i\in[n]

(26)

Proof.

It follows from the fact that
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}
is
0
, the definition of
f
in (
25
) and the choice of the
d_{i}
’s that
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
and the remaining summands of
f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
are degree separated from one another with respect to
x_{0}
(The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.2
). Thus, by Observation
2.2

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(x_{0}^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})).

Thus, it suffices to analyze the sparsity of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s. We now analyze
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
for
i\in[n]
. If
u_{i}=0
, then

\displaystyle Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)}(x_{i}+2)^{d_{2}},\ Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{2i(d_{2}+1)}x_{i}^{d_{2}}.

If
u_{i}=1
, then

\displaystyle Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)}x_{i}^{d_{2}},\ Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{2i(d_{2}+1)}(x_{i}-2)^{d_{2}}.

By the binomial theorem, the choice of the
d_{2}
and Lucas’s theorem (for the finite characteristic case), if
u_{i}=0
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+1
and
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=1
and, if
u_{i}=1
then
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=1
and
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+1
. In either case,

{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+2.

For the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause,
k\in[m]
,the corresponding polynomial
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is as:

R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{k}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}}+(-1)^{u_{j}})^{d_{3}}.

As the multiplicands in
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
do not share any variables,
{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
is the product of the sparsity of the multiplicands. Since
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
, therefore in the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause there exists
j\in C_{k}
such that
a_{k,j}\neq u_{j}
. For that
j
,
(x_{j}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}}+(-1)^{u_{j}})^{d_{3}}=x_{j}^{d_{3}}
. As at least one literal is true in every clause under
{\mathbf{u}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq(d_{3}+1)^{2}
by the choice of
d_{3}
and Lucas’s theorem (for the finite characteristic case). Thus,

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(x_{0}^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq 1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}=s.

∎

The reverse direction.

We leverage the constraints in (
24
) to show that if
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
for some
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
, then
{\mathbf{b}}
is as described in (
26
). Lemma
6.1
shows that
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
. Then, Lemma
6.2
shows that the summands of
f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
are degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
, then
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For all
i\in[n]
,
k\in[m]
,
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
,
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
,
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
are degree separated from one another with respect to
x_{0}
. Also,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
from other
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s, for
i,j\in[n]
and
i\neq j
. Similarly,
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
from
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
for
k,l\in[m]
and
k\neq l
.

\therefore{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(x_{0}^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\text{ by Lemma \ref{Lemma: degree separation-shift}.}

Lemma
6.3
analyses the sparsity of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
. The proof of Lemma
6.4
uses the condition
d_{2}\geq m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+1
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
and
i\in[n]
:

{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq d_{2}+2,

where
Q_{i}
,
Q_{i,1}
and
Q_{i,2}
are as defined in this section. Equality holds if and only if
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}\in\{-1,1\}
. Further, if
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\neq d_{2}+2
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=2d_{2}+2
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Under the given
{\mathbf{b}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+2
holds for all
i\in[n]
.

Lemmas
6.1
,
6.3
and
6.4
together show that
{\mathbf{b}}
is as described in (
26
). Then, Proposition
6.2
uses
d_{3}\geq m
to show how a satisfying assignment for
\psi
can be recovered from
{\mathbf{b}}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

With
{\mathbf{b}}
as described in (
26
),
{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a satisfying assignment for
\psi
.

Proof.

Suppose not; then there exists
k\in[m]
such that the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause,
\lor_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}\oplus a_{k,j})
, in
\psi
is unsatisfied. Since this clause is unsatisfied,
u_{j}=a_{k,j}
for all
j\in C_{k}
. Thus,
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=x_{0}^{k}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}\pm 2)^{d_{3}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=(d_{3}+1)^{3}\geq(m+1)(d_{3}+1)^{2}
, where the equality holds by the fact that
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is a product of linear forms not sharing variables, the binomial theorem, the choice of
d_{3}
and Lucas’s Theorem (for finite characteristic fields), and the inequality holds as
d_{3}\geq m
. By the definition of
f
and
s
, Observations
6.2
and
2.2
, it holds that

\begin{split}{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))&\geq{\mathcal{S}}((x_{0})^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\\
&\geq 1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+(d_{3}+1)^{2}=s+(d_{3}+1)^{2}>s,\end{split}

a contradiction. Thus,
{\mathbf{u}}
is a satisfying assignment for
\psi
.
∎

Construction for characteristic

fields

and

and

,

,

•

Corresponding to

, where

\begin{split}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})&:=Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})+Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}),\ Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)}x_{i}^{d_{2}},Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{2i(d_{2}+1)}(x_{i}+1)^{d_{2}}.\end{split}

•

For the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause,
k\in[m]
, define
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{k}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}+a_{k,j})^{d_{3}}.

Observations

,

.

Observation 6.4
.

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}))\leq 1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}
and
\textnormal{Supp}(f)=4
.

Remarks.

The sparsity of the polynomial output by the reduction over characteristic
2
fields depends on the number of variables which are complemented within a clause. Hence, for the same number of variables
n
and the same number of clauses
m
, the output polynomial corresponding to two different
\psi
’s may have different sparsity. Thus, the reduction is not natural over characteristic
2
fields.

The forward direction.

Let
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
be such that
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
and
f
, as described in (
27
), be the polynomial corresponding to
\psi
. Proposition

shows how
{\mathbf{u}}
can be used to construct a sparsifying vector
{\mathbf{b}}
and has a proof similar to that of Proposition
6.1
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, such that
\psi({\mathbf{u}})=1
. Then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
, where
{\mathbf{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
is defined as follows:

{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}:=0,{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}:=1-u_{i},i\in[n]

(28)

The reverse direction.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
be such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s
. The analysis of the reverse direction in the previous section holds with some changes. Formally, Lemma

holds without any change in its proof, while Lemma

holds with some change in its statement and proof. Thus,
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
. Lemma

analyses
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
, where
i\in[n]
, and its proof is similar to that of Lemma

.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
and
i\in[n]
:

{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+2,

where
Q_{i}
,

and

and

together show that

is as described in (

). Then, Proposition

shows how a satisfying assignment for

and can be proved similarly as Proposition

.

Proposition 6.4
.

With
{\mathbf{b}}
as described in (

),
{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a satisfying assignment for
\psi
.

6.1.1
Choice of degrees

For characteristic
0
fields.

In this case, the inequalities in (
24
) can be converted to equalities. Thus

\begin{split}d_{3}&=m,\ d_{2}=m(m+1)^{2}+1=O(m^{3})\implies s=O(nm^{3}),\\
d_{1}&=4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2=O(nm^{3}).\end{split}

For finite characteristic fields.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=p>0
. If
p>d_{1}
, where the value of
d_{1}
is as in the
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
case, then the
d_{i}
’s are set as per the
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
case. Otherwise
p=O(nm^{3})
. In such a case, we choose the
d_{i}
’s to be of form
p^{k}-1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\begin{split}d_{3}&\leq pm,\ d_{2}\leq pm(d_{3}+1)^{2}+p=O(p^{3}m^{3})\implies s=O(nm^{3}p^{3}),\\
d_{1}&\leq p(4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2)=O(nm^{3}p^{4}).\end{split}

As
p=O(nm^{3})
, therefore

d_{1}=O(n^{5}m^{15}),\ d_{2}=O(n^{3}m^{12}),\ d_{3}=O(nm^{4})\text{ and }s=O(n^{4}m^{12}).

6.2
Hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}

Proof sketch.

Like in Section
4
, for a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
, we carefully analyze the sparsity of the corresponding polynomial
f
as defined in Section
6.1
. For unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, we do a slightly deeper analysis on
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
, for all
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
, to show a lower bound. For satisfiable
\psi
’s,
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
has already been upper bounded for an appropriate
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
. The degree parameters are also chosen differently so that the gap between the lower bound and the upper bound is significant. Comparing the sparsities for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s proves part two of Theorem
4
. Throughout this section, we assume
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
to be an arbitrary constant.

6.2.1
For characteristic
0
fields

For a
\mathrm{3\text{-}CNF}

\psi
, consider the polynomial
f
as defined in (
25
). Choose the
d_{i}
’s to satisfy the following while also being
(mn)^{O(1)}
:

d_{3}\geq\max(4mn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{2}=m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+1,\ d_{1}=d_{2}^{2}+4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2.

(29)

Under these constraints, the conditions in (
24
) are also satisfied. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Then,
s=1+n(2d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}
by Observation
6.3
. For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
6.6
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
for all
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
. In the lemma, Item
1
is essentially Lemma
6.1
, and Item
2
is the analysis in Proposition
6.2
. Thus, Lemma
6.6
encapsulates the analysis of the reverse direction in Section
6.1
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
6.1
, there exists
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
6.5
shows
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by comparing the sparsity for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, and uses Lemma
6.6
and the conditions in (
29
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
,
f
be as defined in (
25
) corresponding to
\psi
and
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
.

1.

If
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}\neq 0
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq\frac{d_{1}}{2}
.

2.

If
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq(d_{3}+1)^{3}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=0
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
{\mathbf{b}}
is as described in (
26
). If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, then it follows from Lemma
6.6
that for any
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq\min\left(\frac{d_{1}}{2},(d_{3}+1)^{3}\right).

The constraints imposed in (
29
) ensure that
(d_{3}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{2}=m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+1
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{2}=3mn(d_{3}+1)^{2}+3n\leq 4mn(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Thus, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and the NO instances is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{4mn(d_{3}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{3}+1}{4mn}.

Also, as
d_{3}\geq 4mn
,
{{\mathcal{S}}(f)}=s\leq 2m(d_{3}+1)^{3}\implies d_{3}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{3}+1}{4mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{3}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{1/3}4m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

6.2.2
Extension to finite characteristic fields

Let the characteristic be
p>0
. If
p>d_{1}
, where
d_{1}
is as chosen in Section
6.2.1
, then the argument of that section continues to hold. Otherwise,
p\leq d_{1}=(mn)^{O(1)}
. In this case, choose the
d_{i}
’s to be of form
p^{k}-1
so that they are
(mn)^{O(1)}
and satisfy the following conditions:

d_{3}\geq\max(3pmn,(mn)^{O(1/\epsilon)}),\ d_{2}>m(d_{3}+1)^{2},\ d_{1}>d_{2}^{2}+4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2.

(30)

We can get
d_{1}=O(p(d_{2}^{2}+4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2))
and
d_{2}=O(pm(d_{3}+1)^{2})
. For this choice of the
d_{i}
’s, the conditions in (
24
) are also satisfied. Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Then,
s=1+n(2d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}
by Observation
6.3
. Note that Lemma
6.6
also holds over finite characteristic fields due to the choice of the
d_{i}
’s and Lucas’s Theorem. Thus, For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
6.6
shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
for all
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
. In the lemma, Item
1
is essentially Lemma
6.1
, and Item
2
is the analysis in Proposition
6.2
. Thus, Lemma
6.6
encapsulates the analysis of the reverse direction in Section
6.1
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
6.1
, there exists
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
6.6
shows
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by comparing the sparsity for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, and uses Lemma
6.6
and the conditions in (
30
).

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=p>2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
6.5
. If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
where
{\mathbf{b}}
is as described in (
26
) and
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, it follows from Lemma
6.6
that for any
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq\min\bigg(\frac{d_{1}}{2},(d_{3}+1)^{3}\bigg).

The conditions imposed in (
30
) ensure that
(d_{3}+1)^{3}>s_{0}
and
(d_{3}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{2}>m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{2}\leq 3pmn(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Consequently, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and NO instances is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{3pmn(d_{3}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{3}+1}{3pmn}.

Also, note that as
d_{3}\geq 3pmn
, therefore
s\leq 2m(d_{3}+1)^{3}\implies d_{3}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{3}+1}{3pmn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{3}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{p2^{1/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

Extension to characteristic
2
fields

Let the characteristic be
2
. Consider the polynomial as defined in (

). Let
s:={\mathcal{S}}(f)
. Now,
s
depends on the number of variables complemented in a clause. To prove the hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}

is required (see the proof of Proposition

), and this can be achieved if there is at least one clause where all the variables are complemented. Thus, assume, without loss of generality, that such a clause exists (see footnote
20
). Choose the
d_{i}
’s to satisfy (
30
) with
p
set to
2
. By Observation
6.4
and the assumption on
\psi
, it holds that

1+n(d_{2}+2)+(d_{3}+1)^{3}\leq s\leq 1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}.

For
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, Lemma
6.6
, with some changes in its statement and proof, shows lower bounds on
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
for all
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
. In the lemma, Item
1
is essentially Lemma
6.1
, and Item
2
is the analysis in Proposition
6.4
. Thus, Lemma
6.6
encapsulates the analysis of the reverse direction in Section
6.1
. For
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, by Proposition
6.3
, there exists
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
, where
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Proposition
6.7
shows
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by comparing the sparsity for satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\psi
’s, and uses Lemma
6.6
and the conditions in (
30
).

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\textnormal{char}(\mathbb{F})=2
. If the input in
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is an
s
-sparse polynomial, then
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
6.5
. If
\psi\in\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
, then
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\leq s_{0}
where
{\mathbf{b}}
is as described in (
28
) and
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. If
\psi\in\overline{\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}}
, it follows from Lemma
6.6
that for any
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
:

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq\min\left(\frac{d_{1}}{2},(d_{3}+1)^{3}\right).

The conditions imposed in (
30
) ensure that
(d_{3}+1)^{3}>s_{0}
and
(d_{3}+1)^{3}
is the minimum. As
d_{2}>m(d_{3}+1)^{2}
, therefore
s_{0}=1+n(d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{2}\leq 3nd_{2}\leq 6mn(d_{3}+1)^{2}
. Consequently, the gap in the sparsities of the YES instances and NO instances is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{6mn(d_{3}+1)^{2}}=\frac{d_{3}+1}{6mn}.

Also, note that as
d_{3}\geq 6mn
, therefore
s\leq 2m(d_{3}+1)^{3}\implies d_{3}+1\geq(\frac{s}{2m})^{1/3}
. Then, the gap is

\frac{(d_{3}+1)^{3}}{s_{0}}\geq\frac{d_{3}+1}{6mn}\geq\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}.

Finally, note that
s\geq d_{3}^{3}
. Thus, for
d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq(mn)^{O(1)}
large enough,

s^{\epsilon}\geq d_{3}^{3\epsilon}\geq{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\implies\frac{s^{1/3}}{2^{4/3}3m^{4/3}n}\geq s^{1/3-\epsilon}.

Hence, the gap is at least
s^{1/3-\epsilon}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
reduces to
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
for
\alpha=s^{1/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

6.3
Comparison with the work of
[
18
]

In this section, we discuss the results by
[
18
]
on
s\text{-to-}(s-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
, the variant of
\mathrm{SETsparse}
as described before Theorem
4
, and on
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
alongwith a brief overview of the proofs, and compare their results with ours.

Result on
s\text{-to-}(s-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
.

The authors of
[
18
]
showed that polynomial solvability over
R
, an integral domain that is
not
a field, reduces to
s\text{-to-}(s-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
, where the input polynomial can be given in the sparse representation or as a circuit. Thus, their reduction also implies
\mathrm{SETsparse}
(considered over
R
) is at least as hard as polynomial solvability. Their reduction proceeds in two stages. In the first stage, they take as input a system of polynomial equations
T
(given as circuits or in the sparse representation) and construct another system of equations
\tilde{T}
, where each equation is an affine form or a degree
2
polynomial with just two monomials, such that a solution of
T
extends to a solution of
\tilde{T}
and a solution of
\tilde{T}
implies a solution of
T
. In the second stage, a polynomial
P
is constructed from the polynomials in
\tilde{T}
by introducing a set of variables disjoint from that of
\tilde{T}
and multiplying each polynomial in
\tilde{T}
with a distinct variable from the set and adding all of them. The polynomial
P
is of degree
3
and can be represented by a depth-
4
circuit. By analysing the sparsity of
P
under translations, they show that
\tilde{T}
(and thus
T
) has a solution if and only if the sparsity of
P
reduces under some translation vector. The construction of
P
and this analysis requires that
R
contain an element with no multiplicative inverse. Hence, the reduction does not hold as is over fields. Over
\mathbb{Z}
, the undecidability of solving Diophantine equations
[
20
,
75
]
further implies the undecidability of
s\text{-to-}(s-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
over
\mathbb{Z}
.

In contrast, we reduce from
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
and show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\mathrm{SETsparse}
over any integral domain
including
fields when the input polynomial in
\mathrm{SETsparse}
is given in the sparse representation.

Result on
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
.

The authors of
[
18
]
showed that for any constant
\alpha>1
,
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard over
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
when the input polynomial is given in the sparse representation and that there exists a constant
\beta>1
such that
\beta^{d}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard over the mentioned algebraic structures when the input polynomial is in
nd
variables, of degree
d
with
d\leq n^{O(1)}
and is given as a circuit. Note that this also shows
\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard over the mentioned algebraic structures. They also showed that
s^{o(1)}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
, where
s
is the sparsity of the input polynomial given in the sparse representation, is undecidable over
\mathbb{Z}
.

To prove the undecidability of
s^{o(1)}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, they show a reduction from polynomial solvability by using the polynomial
P
, with
\sigma:={\mathcal{S}}(P)
, output by the reduction to
\sigma\text{-to-}(\sigma-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
. They observed that
\sigma\text{-to-}(\sigma-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
can be viewed as
\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
where the inputs are a polynomial of sparsity
\sigma
and an integer
\sigma-1
. By multiplying
d
variable-disjoint copies of
P
, where
d
is a positive integer, they get a polynomial
Q
with
s:={\mathcal{S}}(Q)=\sigma^{d}
. By analyzing the sparsity of
Q
under translations, they show a gap of
(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1})^{d}\approx e^{\frac{d}{\sigma-1}}=s^{\frac{1}{(\sigma-1)\log(\sigma)}}=s^{o(1)}
. Thus, they amplify the gap in
\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
to show polynomial solvability reduces to
s^{o(1)}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
, where
s
is the sparsity of the input polynomial, over any integral domain that is
not
a field. Then, the undecidability of
s^{o(1)}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
over
\mathbb{Z}
follows from that of solving Diophantine equations over
\mathbb{Z}
.

To show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
for all constants
\alpha>1
over
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, they first show a reduction from the
(\epsilon,\delta)\text{-}\mathrm{Max}
-
3\mathrm{Lin}
problem
22
22

22

In the
(\epsilon,\delta)\text{-}\mathrm{Max}
-
3\mathrm{Lin}
problem, where
1>\epsilon>\delta>0
, a system of linear equations over the underlying algebraic structure is given as input, where each equation is dependent on exactly
3
variables, and we need to decide if there exists an assignment to the variables such that at least
\epsilon
fraction of the equations are satisfiable or if for all assignments at most
\delta
fraction are satisfiable.
, which is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard over the specified algebraic structures for appropriate
\epsilon
and
\delta
by the PCP theorem
[
49
,
25
,
37
,
38
]
, to
{\beta\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}}\mathrm{SETsparse}
for a small constant
\beta>1
by constructing a polynomial
P
of degree
2
from the system of linear equations given as input in
(\epsilon,\delta)\text{-}\mathrm{Max}
-
3\mathrm{Lin}
. Let
n
be the number of variables in
P
, then
P
can also be represented as a depth-
2
circuit of size
n^{O(1)}
. They then multiply
d
variable-disjoint copies of
P
, where
d
is a parameter, to get an
nd
-variate polynomial
Q
, which has sparsity
{\mathcal{S}}(P)^{d}
if
P
is given in the sparse representation and a circuit of size
Nd+1
if
P
is given as a circuit of size
N
. In particular,
Q
has a depth-
3
(
\Pi\Sigma\Pi
) circuit of size
n^{O(1)}d
. By analyzing the sparsity of
Q
under translations, they amplify the gap
\beta
to
\beta^{d}
and show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
{\beta^{d}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}}\mathrm{SETsparse}
. For any constant
\alpha>1
, taking
d=\log_{\beta}(\alpha)
shows the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
. Their reduction runs in polynomial time as long as
d=O(1)
when
P
is given in the sparse representation, and
d\leq N^{O(1)}
when
P
is given as a circuit of size
N
. Thus,
{\beta^{d}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}}\mathrm{SETsparse}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
d\leq n^{O(1)}
as
P
can be given as a depth-
2
circuit. Note that the reduction from
(\epsilon,\delta)\text{-}\mathrm{Max}
-
3\mathrm{Lin}
does
not
imply the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
s\text{-to-}(s-1)\mathrm{SETsparse}
over the specified algebraic structures because for any translation vector
{\mathbf{b}}
, the lower bound on
{\mathcal{S}}(P({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
is strictly less than
{\mathcal{S}}(P({\mathbf{x}}))
and the same holds for
Q
.

In contrast, we reduce from
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
and show that
{s^{1/3-\epsilon}\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}}\mathrm{SETsparse}
, where
s
is the sparsity of the input polynomial given in the sparse representation, is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard over any integral domain
including
fields. We establish the gap, which is
super-constant
, by a slightly deeper analysis of the sparsity of the polynomial
f
, constructed in Section
6.1
, under translations and comparing the sparsities corresponding to satisfiable and unsatisfiable
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
formulas. Our reduction does
not
use gap amplification or invoke the PCP theorem.

7
Conclusion

In this work, we show that ET for sparse polynomials is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. Particularly, we show the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of MCSP for orbits of homogeneous sparse polynomials (a dense subclass of
\textnormal{hom-}\Sigma\Pi\Sigma
circuits). We also define a gap version of ET for sparse polynomials and show it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, which implies the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
s^{\frac{1}{3}-\epsilon}
-factor approximation of the sparse-orbit complexity of
s
-sparse polynomials. We also show that ET for constant-support polynomials is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard. Lastly, we show that SET for sparse polynomials is also
\mathsf{NP}
-hard along with the hardness of a gap version of the problem. In all four cases, we reduce
\mathrm{3\text{-}SAT}
to the respective problems. We end by listing some problems whose solutions we do not know:

1.

Hardness of
\mathrm{ETsparse}
and
\mathrm{SETsparse}
for constant degree polynomials:
In the reduction of Theorems
1
and
4
, can the degree of the output polynomial be made constant? Currently, the degree is polynomial in the number of clauses and variables.

2.

Improving the gap in Theorem
2
and part two of Theorem
4
:
Can
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{ETsparse}
and
\alpha\text{-}\mathrm{gap}\text{-}\mathrm{SETsparse}
be shown
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
\alpha=s^{1-\epsilon}
, where
s
is the sparsity of the input polynomial and
\epsilon>0
is an arbitrary constant?

3.

Hardness of
\mathrm{ETsupport}
for
\sigma=2
: Is checking if a given polynomial is in the orbit of a support-
2
polynomial
\mathsf{NP}
-hard? Theorem
3
shows that
\mathrm{ETsupport}
for
\sigma\geq 5
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

4.

Hardness of MCSP for
\textnormal{hom-}\Sigma\Pi\Sigma
circuits:
Is MCSP for
\textnormal{hom-}\Sigma\Pi\Sigma
circuits
\mathsf{NP}
-hard?

Appendix A
Handling translations in Theorems
1
and
2

A.1
Extending Theorem
1
for translations

In this section, we modify the construction of Section
3.1
to prove part
1
of Theorem
1
while considering translations. The idea is to choose the degree parameters for the polynomial
f
such that if
f(A{\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is
s
-sparse, where
A\in\mathrm{GL}(|{\mathbf{z}}|,\mathbb{F})
and
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
, then
{\mathbf{b}}
must be
\mathbf{0}
(the all
0
s vector in
\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
) and
A
must be as described in (
4
). We first show the reduction over any field of characteristic not equal to
2
and give a separate construction for characteristic
2
fields.

Formally, let
\psi
,
{\mathbf{x}},x_{0},{\mathbf{y}}
and
{\mathbf{z}}
be as denoted in Section
3.1
. For
{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{F}^{|{\mathbf{z}}|}
,
{\mathbf{b}}_{|w}
denotes the component of
{\mathbf{b}}
corresponding to the variable
w\in{\mathbf{z}}
. Let
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
,
d_{3}
,
d_{4}\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the following polynomials:

•

Corresponding to
x_{i}
, where
i\in[n]
, define
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
as:

\begin{split}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})&:=Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})+Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})+Q_{i,3}({\mathbf{z}}),\ Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{(3i-2)(d_{2}+1)}x_{i}^{d_{2}},\\
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})&:=x_{0}^{(3i-1)(d_{2}+1)}(y_{i}+x_{i})^{d_{3}}\text{ and }Q_{i,3}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{3i(d_{2}+1)}(y_{i}-x_{i})^{d_{3}}.\end{split}

•

For the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
clause,
k\in[m]
, define
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{k(3d_{4}+1)}\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(y_{j}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}}x_{j})^{d_{4}}.

Define
s:=1+n(d_{3}+3)+m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
. Impose the following conditions on the
d_{i}
’s:

d_{1}\geq 6n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{3}+2,\ d_{2}\geq 2d_{3},\ d_{3}\geq m(d_{4}+1)^{2}+1,\ d_{4}\geq m.

(31)

Finally, define
f({\mathbf{z}})
as:

f({\mathbf{z}}):=x_{0}^{d_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}).

(32)

The
d_{i}
’s are chosen in Section
A.1.1
such that they are
(mn)^{O(1)}
and also satisfy the inequalities of (
31
) over any field. Observations
A.1
,
A.2
and
A.3
hold under the conditions of (
31
).

Observation A.1
.

For all
i\in[n]
,
k\in[m]
, the polynomials
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
,
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})
,
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})
,
Q_{i,3}({\mathbf{z}})
and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
are degree separated from one another. Also,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from other
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}})
’s, for
i,j\in[n]
and
i\neq j
. Similarly,
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}})
for
k,l\in[m]
and
k\neq l
.

Proof.

Let
i\in[n]
. Note that
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})
has degree
(3i-2)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}
,
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})
has degree
(3i-1)(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}
and
Q_{i,3}({\mathbf{z}})
has degree
3i(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}
. Clearly,

3i(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}>(3i-1)(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}>(3i-2)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}.

Thus,
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}})
,
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}})
and
Q_{i,3}({\mathbf{z}})
are degree separated and
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is a sum of three degree separated polynomials and has degree
3i(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}
.

Now, let
i\in[n]
and
k\in[m]
. The lowest degree of any monomial of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is
(3i-2)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}>2d_{2}
, while the degree of any monomial of
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is
k(3d_{4}+1)+3d_{4}\leq m(3d_{4}+1)+3d_{4}
. As
d_{2}\geq 2d_{3}
and
d_{3}>m(d_{4}+1)^{2}
from (
31
), therefore

\begin{split}2d_{2}\geq 4d_{3}>4m(d_{4}+1)^{2}>m(3d_{4}+1)+3d_{4}\end{split}

Thus
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
.

Lastly, let
i,j\in[n]
where
i<j
without loss of generality. The highest degree of any monomial of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is
3i(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}
while the lowest degree of any monomial of
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}})
is
(3j-2)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}
. Note that

(3j-2)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}\geq(3i+1)(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}>3i(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}

because
j\geq i+1
and
d_{2}>d_{3}
from (
31
). Therefore
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
and
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}})
are degree separated. That
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}})
for
k,l\in[m]
and
k\neq l
can be observed from the fact that the degree of
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is
k(3d_{4}+1)+3d_{4}
. Clearly,
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
is degree separated from the rest of the polynomials because
d_{1}\geq 6n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{3}+2
while the highest degree polynomial among
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
’s and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
’s is
Q_{n}({\mathbf{z}})
of degree
3n(d_{2}+1)+d_{3}
.

∎

Observation A.2
.

The degree of
f
is
d_{1}=(mn)^{O(1)}
with
\frac{d_{1}}{2}>s
.

Proof.

By the definition of
f
in (
32
) and Observation
A.1
, the degree of
f
