is a sum of two polynomials degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
and has degree
2i(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}
. It is also easy to observe that for
i,j\in[n]
, where
i<j
without loss of generality,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}})
with respect to
x_{0}
. Similarly,
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is degree separated from
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}})
with respect to
x_{0}
, for
k,l\in[m]
and
k\neq l
.

Now, let
i\in[n]
and
k\in[m]
. The
x_{0}
-degree of a monomial of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}})
is at least
(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)\geq d_{2}+1
while that of a monomial of
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})
is
k
. As
d_{2}+1\geq m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+2>m\geq k
by the conditions in (
24
), therefore
Q_{i}
is degree separated from
R_{k}
. Lastly,
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
is degree separated from the rest of the polynomials with respect to
x_{0}
because
d_{1}\geq 4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2
while the highest
x_{0}
-degree of a monomial among
Q_{i}
’s and
R_{k}
’s is
2n(d_{2}+1)
.

F.2
Proof of Observation
6.2

By Observation
6.1
and the definition of
f
in (
25
), the degree of
f
is the maximum degree among
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
,
Q_{i}
’s and
R_{k}
’s, where
i\in[n]
and
k\in[m]
. Now, the degree of
Q_{i}
is
2i(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}
, that of
R_{k}
is
k+3d_{3}
and
2i(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}>k+3d_{3}
. Further,
d_{1}\geq 4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}+2
. Hence, the degree of
f
is
d_{1}
. Finally, it follows from (
24
) that
\frac{d_{1}}{2}>2n(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}>s
.

F.3
Proof of Observation
6.3

By Observations
6.1
and
2.2
,

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}))=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}})).

Let
i\in[n]
. By Observation
2.6
,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1})={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2})=d_{2}+1
. Thus,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i})=2d_{2}+2
. Now, let
k\in[m]
. By Observation
2.6
and the assumption that each clause in
\psi
has
3
distinct variables, we get that:

{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}))={\mathcal{S}}(x_{0}^{k})\prod_{j\in C_{k}}{\mathcal{S}}((x_{j}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}})^{d_{3}})=(d_{3}+1)^{3}\ \ \forall k\in[m].

Thus,

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}))=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}))=1+n(2d_{2}+2)+m(d_{3}+1)^{3}.

This also shows that
{\mathcal{S}}(f)=(mn)^{O(1)}
and
{\mathcal{S}}(f)>s
. For the support of
f
, note that
\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k})=4
for all
k\in[m]
while
\textnormal{Supp}(Q_{i})=2
for all
i\in[n]
and
\textnormal{Supp}(x_{0}^{d_{1}})=1
. By Observation
6.1
,
\textnormal{Supp}(f)=\textnormal{Supp}(R_{k})=4
.

F.4
Proof of Lemma
6.1

Let
b_{0}={\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}
. If
b_{0}\neq 0
, then by the binomial theorem

(x_{0}+b_{0})^{d_{1}}=\sum_{i=0}^{d_{1}}\binom{d_{1}}{i}b_{0}^{i}x_{0}^{d_{1}-i}.

The summands in the above expansion are degree separated and
\binom{d_{1}}{i}\neq 0
for all
i\in[0,d_{1}]
, by the choice of
d_{1}
(and Lucas’s Theorem if the characteristic is finite). Therefore,
(x_{0}+b_{0})^{d_{1}}
contains at least one monomial of degree
i
, for all
i\in[0,d_{1}]
. Since
\frac{d_{1}}{2}>2n(d_{2}+1)+d_{2}>s
therefore at least
s+1
monomials in
(x_{0}+b_{0})^{d_{1}}
are degree separated from
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s with respect to
x_{0}
. Hence
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))>s
, a contradiction. Thus,
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
.

F.5
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Let
i\in[n]
. For
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
and
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
, the
x_{0}
-degree of a monomial in the respective polynomials is
(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)
and
2i(d_{2}+1)
respectively. This clearly implies
Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
and
Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
are degree separated from one another with respect to
x_{0}
. For
i,j\in[n]
, with
i<j
without loss of generality,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is degree separated from
Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
with respect to
x_{0}
as the highest
x_{0}
-degree in the former polynomial is
2i(d_{2}+1)
while the lowest
x_{0}
-degree in the latter polynomial is
(2j-1)(d_{2}+1)\geq(2i+1)(d_{2}+1)
. Let
k,l\in[m]
and
k<l
without loss of generality. For
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
and
R_{l}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
, the
x_{0}
-degree of a monomial in respective polynomials is
k
and
l
implying
R_{k}
and
R_{l}
are also degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
.

Lastly, let
i\in[n]
and
k\in[m]
. The
x_{0}
-degree of a monomial of
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is at least
(2i-1)(d_{2}+1)\geq d_{2}+1
while that of a monomial of
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is
k\leq m
. As
d_{2}+1\geq m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+2>m\geq k
by the conditions in (
24
). Therefore,
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
is degree separated from
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s with respect to
x_{0}
. Clearly,
x_{0}^{d_{1}}
is degree separated with respect to
x_{0}
from
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
because
d_{1}>4n(d_{2}+1)+2d_{2}
, while the highest
x_{0}
-degree of any monomial among the
Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})^{\prime}s
and
R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})
’s is
2n(d_{2}+1)
.

F.6
Proof of Lemma
6.3

From Lemma
6.2
and Observation
2.2
it follows that
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))
. The if direction of the lemma statement is easy to verify. For the only if direction consider the following cases of
{\mathbf{b}}
:

1.

{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}\notin\{-1,1\}
: In this case,
x_{i}+{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}-1
and
x_{i}+{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}+1
both have a non-zero constant. Then, by the binomial theorem, the choice of
d_{2}
and Lucas’s Theorem (for finite characteristic fields), it holds that
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+1
and
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+1
implying
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=2d_{2}+2
.

2.

{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}\in\{-1,1\}
: In this case, exactly one of
x_{i}+{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}-1
and
x_{i}+{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}+1
has a non-zero constant. Without loss of generality, let
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{i}}=1
. Then,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,2}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+1
(by arguing as in the first case) while
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i,1}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=1
implying
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+2
.

It is clear from the above cases that if
{\mathbf{b}}
is not as per the lemma statement, then
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=2d_{2}+2
; otherwise,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=d_{2}+2
.

F.7
Proof of Lemma
6.4

Suppose
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\neq d_{2}+2
for some
j\in[n]
. Then,
{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=2d_{2}+2
by Lemma
6.3
. By the definition of
f
, Lemma
6.2
and the condition
d_{2}\geq m(d_{3}+1)^{2}+1
, we get the following contradiction:

\begin{split}{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))&>{\mathcal{S}}((x_{0})^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{j}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\\
&\geq 1+(n-1)(2+d_{2})+(2+2d_{2})=1+n(2+d_{2})+d_{2}>s.\end{split}

F.8
Proof of Lemma
6.6

If
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}\neq 0
, then it follows from the argument in the proof of Lemma
6.1
that
{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))\geq\frac{d_{1}}{2}
. Henceforth, we assume
{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{0}}=0
. Then, by Lemma
6.2
, it follows that

{\mathcal{S}}(f({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))={\mathcal{S}}(x_{0}^{d_{1}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{S}}(Q_{i}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})).

Now, note that

R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}})=\prod_{j\in C_{k}}(x_{j}+{\mathbf{b}}_{|x_{j}}+(-1)^{a_{k,j}})^{d_{3}}.

As
\psi
is unsatisfiable, there exists a
k\in[m]
such that
{\mathcal{S}}(R_{k}({\mathbf{z}}+{\mathbf{b}}))=(d_{3}+1)^{3}
, which follows by the binomial theorem.
